We now discuss the relation between antipodes and our base additive category having
negatives
. Following
[
2
,
12
]
, we say that an additive (symmetric monoidal) category
\mathbb{X}
has
negatives
if each homset
\mathbb{X}(A,B)
is in fact an Abelian group. Explicitly, this means that for every map
f:A\to B
, there is a map
{-f:A\to B}
such that
f+(-f)=0
. As is common, we write
f-g
to express
f+(-g)
. Of course, an additive (symmetric monoidal) category with negatives is equivalently a (symmetric monoidal) category enriched over Abelian groups. As such, this means that composition (and the monoidal product) preserves negatives:

\displaystyle f;(-g);h=-\left(f;g;h\right)

\displaystyle f;(g-h);k=f;g;k-f;h;k

(158)

\begin{gathered}f\otimes(-g)=-(f\otimes g)=(-f)\otimes g\\
f\otimes(g-h)=(f\otimes g)-(f\otimes h)\qquad(f-g)\otimes k=(f\otimes k)-(g\otimes k)\end{gathered}

(159)

In particular, note that:

\displaystyle f;-1_{B}=-f=-1_{A};f

(160)

So an additive category having negatives is completely determined by whether identity morphisms have inverses with respect to the sum. In fact, an additive symmetric monoidal category having negatives is completely determined by if the identity for the monoidal unit has an inverse with respect to the sum
[
12
, Lemma 7.5]
. Thus, this justifies that in string diagrams we draw:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](427)at(86.5,1.25){};\node[style=port](428)at(86.5,-0.75){};\node[style=component](429)at(86.5,0.25){$-f$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](427)to(429);\draw[style=wire](429)to(428);\end{array}=-\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](427)at(86.5,1.25){};\node[style=port](428)at(86.5,-0.75){};\node[style=component](429)at(86.5,0.25){$f$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](427)to(429);\draw[style=wire](429)to(428);\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](427)at(86.5,1.25){};\node[style=port](428)at(86.5,-0.75){};\node[style=component](429)at(86.5,0.25){$f-g$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](427)to(429);\draw[style=wire](429)to(428);\end{array}=\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](427)at(86.5,1.25){};\node[style=port](428)at(86.5,-0.75){};\node[style=component](429)at(86.5,0.25){$f$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](427)to(429);\draw[style=wire](429)to(428);\end{array}-\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](427)at(86.5,1.25){};\node[style=port](428)at(86.5,-0.75){};\node[style=component](429)at(86.5,0.25){$g$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](427)to(429);\draw[style=wire](429)to(428);\end{array}

We can now extend Thm
7
and say that a monoidal Hopf coalgebra modality with a pre-codereliction induces an Abelian group enrichment.

Proposition 17
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I},\nabla,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{S})
be a monoidal Hopf coalgebra modality with a pre-codereliction
\eta
on a symmetric monoidal category
\mathbb{X}
. Then for the additive enrichment as defined in Thm
7
,
\mathbb{X}
is an additive symmetric monoidal category with negatives where for a map
f:A\to B
,
-f:A\to B
is equal to following composite:

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}_{A}}
\textstyle{\oc(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oc(f)}
\textstyle{\oc(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{B}}
\textstyle{B}

which is drawn as follows:

\displaystyle-\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](427)at(86.5,1.25){};\node[style=port](428)at(86.5,-0.75){};\node[style=component](429)at(86.5,0.25){$f$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](427)to(429);\draw[style=wire](429)to(428);\end{array}:=\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](53)at(139,1.25){};\node[style=component](54)at(139,-0.25){$\mathsf{S}$};\node[style=port](55)at(139,-3){};\node[style=component](56)at(139,-1.25){$f$};\node[style=none](57)at(138.5,-0.75){};\node[style=none](58)at(139.5,-0.75){};\node[style=none](59)at(138.5,-1.75){};\node[style=none](60)at(139.5,-1.75){};\node[style=component](61)at(139,0.5){$\eta$};\node[style=component](62)at(139,-2.25){$\varepsilon$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](57.center)to(58.center);\draw[style=wire](58.center)to(60.center);\draw[style=wire](60.center)to(59.center);\draw[style=wire](59.center)to(57.center);\draw[style=wire](54)to(56);\draw[style=wire](56)to(62);\draw[style=wire](62)to(55);\draw[style=wire](61)to(54);\draw[style=wire](53)to(61);\end{array}

So we conclude that the base additive symmetric monoidal category has negatives.

Let us now revisit additive bialgebra modalities in additive symmetric monoidal categories with negatives. We first show that in the presence of negatives, convolution bialgebra modalities have antipodes.

Lemma 18
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\nabla,\mathsf{u})
be a convolution bialgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category with negatives. Then
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\nabla,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{S})
is a Hopf coalgebra modality where
\mathsf{S}_{A}:=\oc(-1_{A})
, which is drawn as follows:

Proof 7.2
.

We first show that the equalities of (
18

We now give an alternative proof of
[
12
, Prop 7.6]
, which says that for an additive bialgebra modality the converse of Lemma
18
is true.

{lemC}

[
[

, Prop 7.6]
] Let

be an additive bialgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category

. Then

admits negatives if and only if
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\nabla,\mathsf{u})
has an antipode
\mathsf{S}

So we conclude that the base additive symmetric monoidal category has negatives.

As an immediate consequence, it follows that in the presence of negatives, any additive/monoidal bialgebra modality is a monoidal Hopf coalgebra modality.

Corollary 19
.

Let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I},\nabla,\mathsf{u})
be a monoidal bialgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category with negatives. Then
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I},\nabla,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{S})
is a monoidal Hopf coalgebra modality, where
\mathsf{S}_{A}:=\oc(-1_{A})
.

Proof 7.4
.

By Lemma
18
, we know that
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\nabla,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{S})
is a Hopf coalgebra modality. Then since the antipode is of the form
\mathsf{S}_{A}:=\oc(-1_{A})
, (
7
) is precisely the naturality of
\delta
(
2
), while (
7
) follows from the naturality of
\mathsf{m}
(
2
) and (
159
). Thus we conclude that
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I},\nabla,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{S})
is a monoidal Hopf coalgebra modality, as desired.

Moreover, since antipodes are unique, for any monoidal Hopf coalgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category with negatives, its antipode must be given as in (
18
).

Corollary 20
.

If
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I},\nabla,\mathsf{u},\mathsf{S})
is a monoidal Hopf coalgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category with negatives, then
\mathsf{S}_{A}=\oc(-1_{A})
.

8.
Differential Linear Categories Revisited

In this final section, we bring everything together to provide a new axiomatization of a differential linear category. For an in-depth introduction to differential categories, we invite the reader to see
[
3
,
2
,
7
,
9
,
11
]
.

It may be worthwhile to first recall the definition of a differential category in terms of a
deriving transformation
, as was originally done in
[
3
]
. Borrowing terminology from
[
9
]
, a
differential modality

[
9
, Def 4.3]
on an additive symmetric monoidal category is a sextuple
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{d})
consisting of a coalgebra modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e})
and a natural transformation
\mathsf{d}_{A}:\oc(A)\otimes A\to\oc(A)
such that the diagrams in Appendix
A.16
commute. The natural transformation
\mathsf{d}
is called the
deriving transformation
and is drawn as follows
6
6

6

We note that we are drawing the deriving transformation differently then how it was drawn in
[
2
]
for clarity.
:

(186)

The axioms of a deriving transformation capture the fundamental identities from differential calculus. We call
[D.1]
the constant rule,
[D.2]
the Leibniz rule,
[D.2]
the linear rule,
[D.4]
the chain rule, and
[D.5]
the interchange rule. See
[
3
,
2
]
for more intuition on the axioms of a deriving transformation. Then a
differential category

[
2
, Def 7]
is an additive symmetric monoidal category with a differential modality.

A
monoidal differential modality

[
9
, Def 4.3]
on an additive symmetric monoidal category is an octuple
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I},\mathsf{d})
consisting of a monoidal coalgebra modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I})
and a differential modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{d})
. Now one might expect that the definition of a monoidal differential modality should also require some compatibility axioms between the deriving transformation and the monoidal structure, or even the induced bialgebra structure. However, one of the key results from
[
2
]
is that these compatibilities come for free for a monoidal differential modality. Thus for a monoidal differential modality, the diagrams in Appendix
A.17
commute
[
2
, Cor 7]
, which are drawn as follows:

where
[D.
\nabla
]
is called the
\nabla
-rule
[
2
, Def 8]
and
[D.m.r]
(resp.
[D.m.l]
) is called the right (resp. left) monoidal rule
[
2
, Sec 6]
. The monoidal rule was also called the strength rule in
[
8
, Def 4.2]
. We also note that in the string diagrams for the monoidal rule, the deriving transformation is of type
\mathsf{d}_{A\otimes B}:\oc(A\otimes B)\otimes A\otimes B\to\oc(A\otimes B)
, and is therefore drawn as taking in three inputs. Then a
differential linear category

[
2
, Sec 6]
is an additive symmetric monoidal category with a monoidal differential modality. Examples of differential (linear) categories can be found in
[
2
, Sec 9]
and
[
9
, Ex 4.7]
. In particular, every free exponential modality is a monoidal differential modality, and thus every additive Lafont category is a differential linear category
[
12
, Thm 21]
.

One of the fundamental theorems about differential linear categories is that the differential structure can also be axiomatized in terms of
coderelictions
, which lines up more nicely with the Differential Linear Logic perspective
[
7
]
. Using the terminology of this paper, a
codereliction

[
2
, Def 9]
for a monoidal coalgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category is a pre-codereliction
\eta
such that the diagrams from Appendix
266
also commute, which are drawn as follows:

For a codereliction, the axiom
[cd.1]
is called the constant rule,
[cd.2]
is called the Leibniz (or product) rule, and
[cd.4]
is called the chain rule
8
8

8

In
[
2
, Sec 5]
, this version of
[cd.4]
was called the
alternative
chain rule. However here we follow Fiore’s approach
[
8
]
and the more recent convention of calling
[cd.4]
the chain rule for the codereliction.
.

We now briefly review the bijective correspondence between coderelictions and deriving transformations, that is, how we can build one from the other, as given in Appendix
A.18
. So let
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I})
be a monoidal coalgebra modality on an additive symmetric monoidal category, with induced additive bialgebra modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\nabla,\mathsf{u})
. Then given a codereliction
\eta
, define
\mathsf{d}
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](192)at(158.75,3.75){};\node[style=component](193)at(159.5,1.5){$\mathsf{d}$};\node[style=none](194)at(158.75,2.25){};\node[style=none](195)at(160.25,2.25){};\node[style=port](196)at(160.25,3.75){};\node[style=port](198)at(159.5,0.5){};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](193)to(194.center);\draw[style=wire](193)to(195.center);\draw[style=wire](198)to(193);\draw[style=wire](192)to(194.center);\draw[style=wire](196)to(195.center);\end{array}:=\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](95)at(161.5,3.75){};\node[style=component](96)at(162.25,1.5){$\nabla$};\node[style=none](97)at(161.5,2.25){};\node[style=none](98)at(163,2.25){};\node[style=port](99)at(163,3.75){};\node[style=component](100)at(163,3){$\eta$};\node[style=port](105)at(162.25,0.5){};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](96)to(97.center);\draw[style=wire](96)to(98.center);\draw[style=wire](100)to(99);\draw[style=wire](98.center)to(100);\draw[style=wire](105)to(96);\draw[style=wire](95)to(97.center);\end{array}

Then
\mathsf{d}
is a deriving transformation
[
2
, Thm 3]
. Conversely, given instead a deriving transformation
\mathsf{d}
, define
\eta
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](76)at(156.25,-1){};\node[style=component](78)at(156.25,0.5){$\eta$};\node[style=port](79)at(156.25,2){};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](76)to(78);\draw[style=wire](78)to(79);\end{array}:=\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](95)at(163,3.75){};\node[style=component](96)at(162.25,1.5){$\mathsf{d}$};\node[style=none](97)at(163,2.25){};\node[style=none](98)at(161.5,2.25){};\node[style=component](100)at(161.5,3){$\mathsf{u}$};\node[style=port](105)at(162.25,0.5){};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](96)to(97.center);\draw[style=wire](96)to(98.center);\draw[style=wire](98.center)to(100);\draw[style=wire](105)to(96);\draw[style=wire](95)to(97.center);\end{array}

Then
\eta
is a codereliciton
[
2
, Thm 3]
. Moreover, these constructions are inverses of each other. As such for monoidal coalgebra modalities, there is a bijective correspondence between coderelictions and deriving transformations
[
2
, Thm 4]
. Thus, a differential linear category is equivalently an additive symmetric monoidal category with a monoidal coalgebra modality that comes equipped with a codereliction.

Now it turns out that two of the axioms of a codereliction are in fact redundant, that is, provable. Indeed, in an additive enriched setting, the constant rule
[cd.1]
is provable from simply naturality
[
2
, Lemma 6]
, while the product rule
[cd.2]
is provable from naturality and the linear rule
[cd.3]
. Therefore, a codereliction is equivalently simply a natural transformation which satisfies the linear rule
[cd.3]
, the chain rule
[cd.4]
, and either monoidal rule
[cd.m.r/l]

[
2
, Cor 5]
. In other words, a codereliction is a pre-codereliction which also satisfies the chain rule
[cd.4]
. However, observe that the three remaining axioms only require the monoidal bialgebra modality structural maps and no longer seems to require sums or zeros. Thus we can technically define a codereliction for a monoidal bialgebra modality on a symmetric monoidal category to be a pre-codereliction on the underlying monoidal coalgebra modality which also satisfies the chain rule
[cd.4]
. However, it follows from Thm
7
that this gives us an equivalent characterization of a differential linear category without including additive enrichment in the axiomatization. So bringing all of this together, we obtain:

Theorem 21
.

A differential linear category is equivalently a symmetric monoidal category with a monoidal bialgebra modality equipped with a codereliction.

It may also be of interest to ask how, in the presence of negatives, the deriving transformation/codereliction interacts with the antipode.

Proposition 22
.

For a monoidal differential modality
(\oc,\delta,\varepsilon,\Delta,\mathsf{e},\mathsf{m},\mathsf{m}_{I},\mathsf{d})
, with induced codereliction
\eta
, on an additive symmetirc monoidal category with negatives, for the induced antipode
\mathsf{S}
, the diagrams in Appendix
22
commute, which are drawn as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=component](362)at(208.5,1.5){$\mathsf{S}$};\node[style=port](363)at(208,3.75){};\node[style=component](364)at(208.5,2.5){$\mathsf{d}$};\node[style=none](365)at(208,3){};\node[style=none](366)at(209,3){};\node[style=port](367)at(209,3.75){};\node[style=port](368)at(208.5,0.75){};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](364)to(365.center);\draw[style=wire](364)to(366.center);\draw[style=wire](365.center)to(363);\draw[style=wire](366.center)to(367);\draw[style=wire](362)to(364);\draw[style=wire](362)to(368);\end{array}=-\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](213)at(205.75,3.75){};\node[style=port](356)at(206.75,3.75){};\node[style=component](357)at(206.25,1.75){$\mathsf{d}$};\node[style=none](358)at(206.75,2.5){};\node[style=none](359)at(205.75,2.5){};\node[style=component](360)at(205.75,3){$\mathsf{S}$};\node[style=port](361)at(206.25,0.75){};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](357)to(358.center);\draw[style=wire](357)to(359.center);\draw[style=wire](359.center)to(360);\draw[style=wire](361)to(357);\draw[style=wire](356)to(358.center);\draw[style=wire](213)to(360);\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](16)at(-2.25,0.5){};\node[style=component](17)at(-2.25,1.5){$\mathsf{S}$};\node[style=port](18)at(-2.25,3.75){};\node[style=component](19)at(-2.25,2.75){$\eta$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](17)to(16);\draw[style=wire](18)to(19);\draw[style=wire](19)to(17);\end{array}=-\begin{array}[c]{c}\tikzpicture\pgfonlayer{nodelayer}\node[style=port](427)at(86.5,1.25){};\node[style=port](428)at(86.5,-0.75){};\node[style=component](429)at(86.5,0.25){$\eta$};\pgfonlayer{edgelayer}\draw[style=wire](427)to(429);\draw[style=wire](429)to(428);\end{array}

Proof 8.1
.

Recall from Cor
19
that the antipode is of the form
\mathsf{S}_{A}:=\oc(-1_{A})
. Then the equality on the left follows from naturality of
\mathsf{d}
(
8
) and (
159
), while the equality on the right follows from naturality of
\eta
(
3
).

We conclude this paper by discussing uniqueness of coderelictions. In
[
13
, Thm 21]
, it was shown that for free exponential modalities, coderelictions were unique. Here however, we can now extend this and state that for any monoidal coalgebra modality, coderelictions are unique.

Theorem 23
.

For a monoidal coalgebra modality (equivalently an additive bialgebra modality or a monoidal bialgebra modality) on additive symmetric monoidal category, if a codereliction exists, then it is unique.

Proof 8.2
.

In Prop
4
we showed that a pre-codereliciton, if it exists, was unique. Thus since a codereliction is a pre-codereliction by definition, it follows that if one exists, it must be unique.

Moreover, since there is a bijective correspondence between coderelictions and deriving transformations, we also have uniqueness of deriving transformation for monoidal coalgebra modalities.

Corollary 24
.

For a monoidal coalgebra modality on additive symmetric monoidal category, if a deriving transformation exists, then it is unique.

Thus we are properly justified in saying that in categorical models of Differential Linear Logic, there really is only one way to differentiate smooth maps. However, it is important to stress that the above corollary does not necessarily extend to mere coalgebra modalities. Indeed, it is still an open question whether or not deriving transformations are unique for non-monoidal coalgebra modalities.
